### 2026-10-21 — file reads do not explain the later XPR mutation

Instrumented `NtReadFile` only when its destination overlapped `0x810D9000..0x810DB800`. The corrected-async run recorded exactly one overlapping read: caller `0x0018E104` read `0x2800` bytes from file offset `0x00259600` into `0x810D9000`, returned all bytes synchronously, and left the header valid (`00040001,0,0,07710C2A,0`). No later file read overlapped the XPR allocation before the 110-second watchdog at 515 flips. In this runtime, `RECOMP_ASYNC_IO` changes the returned status for asynchronous handles, but `xbox_NtReadFile` completes the actual Windows read before `NtReadFile` returns; this path therefore provides no evidence for a concurrent file-write race. Restored the runtime source, rebuilt Release, and removed the temporary trace marker from the executable. Capture is ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-xpr-file-read-overlap-20261021/`. The next investigation should focus on guest memory writes/resource APIs outside `NtReadFile`.

### 2026-10-21 — overlay XPR resource and registration boundary traced

Ghidra shows that overlay global `DAT_0066A600` is resource-table entry 10. XBE loader `FUN_000C8540(10)` reads an XPR0 block from file offset `0x00259600`, length `0x2800`, into buffer `0x810D9000`, then stores `buffer+0xC` (`0x810D900C`) as the resource pointer. The bytes begin with `XPR0`; before registration the object fields are `00040001, 0, 0, 07710C2A, 0`. This identifies the asset-loading path and shows that the original file data is not the reserved `0x00FFFFFD` pattern.

A bounded runtime trace confirmed `D3DResource_Register` (`0x00211700`) receives that object and data argument `0x00040000`; it changes the object's data field to `0x010D9800` while preserving format `0x07710C2A`. Later `D3DDevice_SetTexture` calls in the same run also read valid object fields (`00040001, 010D9800, 0000076D/771/775, 07710C2A, 0`). The run reached 463 flips at 29.11 FPS before stopping. This conflicts with the earlier capture where the same pointer was reported with repeated `0x00FFFFFD` fields and emitted that reserved state to the pushbuffer. The issue is therefore not resolved: capture the object contents, SetTexture arguments, and pushbuffer write together at the moment of any invalid emission before assigning blame to the renderer, resource loader, or game data.

The exact-address and nearby-address checks had no `D3DDevice_CreateTexture2` or `XGSetTextureHeader` constructor hit, consistent with this XPR file-loading path. Public tooling review confirmed [XboxDev/ghidra-xbe](https://github.com/XboxDev/ghidra-xbe) supports XBE loading and symbol recovery and is already installed for this analysis. [XboxXBERecomp](https://github.com/TGP482/XboxXBERecomp) is a separate MIT-licensed static recompiler with a D3D12 path, but its documented setup does not make it a drop-in for this title's XDK 4627 image. ### 2026-10-21 — upstream renderer review and measured title draw cost

Followed the slow-frame lead into the pinned XboxRecomp pushbuffer executor and compared it with the public v0.15.0 work and the maintainer's current Half-Life 2 renderer notes. The public notes describe the same partial-executor boundary: the CPU path handles a constrained subset of guest draws, while real vertex-program and texture behavior needs a renderer. The current XboxRecomp line has a threaded software raster path, but Steel Battalion's local patch intentionally defaults that pool to one worker because the measured 640×480 splash passes regressed with more workers. The v0.15.0 renderer work is not a drop-in fix for this pinned, customized target. A separate runtime summary in that capture reported 1.06 FPS at 171 flips, 961 vertex-program batches, 1,922 triangles, and 125,794,944 pixel writes. The single-frame trace showed a mix of full-screen 1024×1024 and 512×512 textures plus many 128×256 text batches. These measurements include instrumentation and are not clean frame-time benchmarks; they identify the CPU raster path as a concrete performance lead, not a campaign performance result.

No renderer or runtime behavior changed. Existing Vulkan fast paths have narrow exact-state gates; the captured batches do not establish pixel-equivalence for broader GPU dispatch, so do not widen those gates based on texture dimensions alone. ### 2026-10-21 — DCF4 producer stalls while the DMA queue is idle

Extended the existing bounded DCF4 logger for one diagnostic build, then ran the XPP-seeded startup with event tracing and a native watchdog stack. The title prompt appeared, presentation stopped at 233 flips, and the runtime reported 0 FPS. The log captured 23 `KeSetEvent` calls followed by 24 waits on `0x0021DCF4`; no later set appeared before the 110-second watchdog. USER DMA PUT and GET both remained `0x00004018` at shutdown, so this reproduction ended with the queue drained rather than backed up.

The watchdog's native stack places the main guest thread in `NtWaitForSingleObject` through `xbox_KeWaitForSingleObject` and guest `sub_00211E10`. The existing XboxRecomp [`tools/stackwalk.py`](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/blob/main/tools/stackwalk.py) resolves its guest return chain. The last event setter was host thread 39228 and the waiter was 62656. During the frozen interval Windows reported the setter in `Wait / ExecutionDelay` and the waiter in `Wait / UserRequest`; setter CPU time changed little while no new set call appeared. This is evidence that the event producer stopped making progress in this run, not an explanation of why. A prior XPP-seeded run recorded 162 sets and 163 waits, so the producer's behavior is intermittent.

The event-log limit was raised only in the local diagnostic build, then the runtime source was restored byte-for-byte and the normal XPP-seeded Release target rebuilt successfully. No production behavior, game data, rendering state, or controller mapping changed. Logs and captures remain ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-xpp-event-queue-correlation-20261021/` and `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-xpp-setter-thread-state-20261021/`; no game process remains. ### 2026-10-21 — timer and interrupt progress during slow presentation

The setter thread's native watchdog sample was in `kernel_timer_thread`'s `SleepEx`, which is the expected 10 ms pause in its timer loop; by itself that sample did not indicate a stuck thread. In a separate 60-second trace, the timer loop continued through vblank, IRQ polling, and DPC draining past loop 3,400, even though DCF4 stopped after 23 sets / 24 waits and the DMA queue remained drained at PUT=GET=`0x00006040`. That run reached the title prompt, then stopped presenting at 234 flips.

A further 50-second run with NV2A IRQ tracing recorded vblank ticks through 1,680, repeatedly claimed the asserted NV2A interrupt, and recorded 76 DCF4 sets / 77 waits. USER DMA PUT=GET=`0x0000502C` at the watchdog. Runtime-reported presentation declined from 13.88 FPS at 187 flips to 5.81 at 248 and 3.06 at 284. No framebuffer capture was enabled in this run, so title-screen arrival is not claimed. The pair of runs shows that timer/IRQ progress and DCF4 signaling can continue while presentation slows, while another run can stop signaling with the timer loop still active. This makes a single lost-event or outstanding-queue explanation insufficient; the cause of slow rendering and the intermittent signal gap remain open.

Temporary watchdog-PC and trace-cap edits were restored byte-for-byte, and the XPP-seeded Release target rebuilt successfully from the normal sources. No game process remains. Logs are ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-xpp-setter-pc-20261021/`, `m3-xpp-timer-loop-extended-20261021/`, and `m3-xpp-nv2a-irq-progress-20261021/`. Progress bars remain unchanged. GitHub research found XboxRecomp's merged [PR #228](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/pull/228), which queues small triangles and sends each batch to the raster worker pool. Its author reports Fallout: Brotherhood of Steel's first-level throughput improving from 5.5 to about 15 FPS after a test that showed workers idle while the executor thread was saturated. The local production checkout remains pinned to v0.13.1; the current [v0.15.0 release](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/releases/tag/v0.15.0) includes that work. A compatibility audit found that the worker-pool changes mostly apply to a clean v0.13.1 tree, but the final flush point depends on a newer render-batch structure; the upstream regression fixture also expects a backend header absent from v0.13.1. The temporary clean-tree build therefore did not complete its test, and no raster change was applied to the production checkout. This is a promising performance lead, not a verified Steel Battalion improvement. Keep the existing graphics API and project architecture unchanged while resolving the title boot/wait issue.

It applies cleanly to the pinned v0.13.1 source.

The Windows x64 Release target rebuilt successfully. A 90-second run still reached the dark title screen, with 159 flips at the final sample and about 0.12 FPS. The renderer no longer counts those two clear-rectangle methods as unhandled (155 to 153 distinct); the full scene is still incomplete and no input was sent in this capture. The before/after screenshots show the same title composition because the observed clear rectangle spans the full surface. This fixes method coverage but does not resolve the frame stall or title action handling. ### 2026-10-20 — upstream NV2A method and reuse review

Its dispatcher records `0x0394` and `0x0398` into Z clip registers, and maps `0x1E74` / `0x1E78` into shader-control bits. These are GPU state writes, not queue commands; the title capture alone does not show that they cause the guest wait. The relevant names and addresses are in [xemu's NV2A register header](https://github.com/xemu-project/xemu/blob/master/hw/xbox/nv2a/nv2a_regs.h#L1030-L1037) and the method handlers in [xemu's PGRAPH source](https://github.com/xemu-project/xemu/blob/master/hw/xbox/nv2a/pgraph/pgraph.c#L1604-L1610).

The complete xemu PGRAPH/Vulkan backend is not a drop-in renderer for this static-recomp runtime: its dispatcher and renderer rely on QEMU device state, guest-memory integration, locks, and events. The checkout is research-only and ignored; no xemu code was copied. Its file headers show a mix of LGPL and GPL licensing, so any future direct reuse needs a separate component and license audit.

Other `sub_0022202D` calls in the same trace returned success; they are separate calls and do not negate the DirectSound failure. A small isolated candidate patch models that XDK D3D method as a write to `PGRAPH_PATT_COLOR0` (`0x400B10`) and traces matching reads/writes behind `RECOMP_PB_FENCE_TRACE`; it compiled in the Windows runtime but was not exercised by this run.

### 2026-10-20 — D3D frame-counter registration and follow-up stall

Used the native-PC watchdog and seeded static C to trace the startup wait to `sub_0002DB50` → `sub_0020F2C0`. The pinned runtime already had an NV2A frame-counter service, but no caller registered a counter. Added the Steel Battalion device/counter pair to the local Windows host entry point and preserved it as `patches/steel-battalion-frame-counter-registration.patch`.

A 45-second Vulkan run advanced the guest counter to `0x166`, rendered 628 logo draws, and reached 267 flips, so counter registration changed the observed startup path. It did not reach the title prompt. A 120-second run reached 262 flips, then stopped drawing; the final watchdog was in a `KeWaitForSingleObject` path from the D3D ring-space helper, with the USER DMA queue drained at PUT=GET=`0x00062A28`. The guest-created event was `0x0021DCF4`, while the counter had advanced to `0xD37`; this is a later, distinct wait and shows the registration is not a complete boot fix. The 120-second trace reported 6,312 unhandled NV2A methods (155 distinct), which is now a more direct compatibility lead. Do not treat the idle timer's counter advancement as proof of accurate game timing or successful rendering.

A separate 30-second diagnostic with the runtime's opt-in `RECOMP_TITLE_KEVENTS=1` saw a title-built notification event at `0x0021DCE4` with signal state 0; no set was captured. This was a different startup path and does not explain the D3D event at `0x0021DCF4`. The watchdog native PC and stack, not an event address alone, must distinguish them.

### 2026-10-20 — upstream rasterizer research follow-up

Checked current XboxRecomp performance work against the local v0.13.1 renderer. Upstream PR [#228](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/pull/228) has merged a worker-pool change that queues all triangles in one batch before dispatching rows to workers. The local `nv2a_pb_exec.c` already parallelizes each individual triangle, but it does not queue a batch's triangles together. Upstream reported a 5.5→about 15 FPS improvement on Fallout: Brotherhood of Steel; its test covers overlap order, which must remain stable for correct pixels. This is a relevant candidate for later gameplay rendering, not a verified Steel Battalion improvement.

Upstream PR [#231](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/pull/231) proposes pipelining raster jobs behind pushbuffer decoding. It remains open and requires per-job copies of render state plus explicit drain points before synchronization and surface operations. The reported test was also Fallout: Brotherhood of Steel, with about 15.5→17.4 FPS; the author notes it had not been run on several other titles. It is not a safe drop-in change for the current Steel Battalion bring-up.

These two changes do not directly explain the measured 512×512 splash cost: that draw is only a pair of large triangles, so batching triangles would reduce worker handoffs but would not remove per-pixel combiner, blend, texture, and depth work. Do not import the open pipeline patch or claim either upstream result for Steel Battalion. Next, profile the current windowed build with the established boot flags and identify which exact splash fast-path gate or per-pixel operation accounts for the time; preserve the NV2A reference path for parity. ### 2026-10-20 — windowed startup profile and wait trace

Reused the existing DSP-enabled Release x64 executable with the established boot flags and a real Vulkan window. The first textured logo submission took 36.3 ms including one-time setup; repeated logo submissions measured 7.7–8.7 ms. The capture showed the Dolby startup logo. A later 60-second profile reached 121 flips and a reported 7.64 FPS sample, then the guest stopped advancing DMA at `0x000355A4`; its first screenshot/FPS sample was much slower, so this is not a stable frame-rate result. These results show the repeated splash draw is no longer the 1.1-second headless CPU fallback, but they do not establish title arrival or gameplay.

A 25-second watchdog sample with wait tracing confirmed that the first observed wait on title-created event `0x0021DCE4` returned `STATUS_SUCCESS`. The main guest thread was subsequently inside the XBE's streamed-file read path while `Media\\title\\snk_005.sgt` and RIFF chunks were being read. A separate guest thread made repeated two-object waits that returned `STATUS_TIMEOUT`; this is not proof of a failed file completion. A paired 25-second run with `RECOMP_ASYNC_IO` disabled stalled earlier at one flip, so disabling async I/O is not a demonstrated fix and the runs diverged too early for a fair speed comparison. No code was changed.

Upstream [PR #226](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/pull/226) addresses a file-I/O APC stack restoration bug and offers opt-in deferred APC delivery for titles that use `ReadFileEx` with alertable waits. The local runtime already restores callback registers/ESP around inline callbacks. A temporary opt-in trace rebuilt and ran the windowed diagnostic for 25 seconds: it recorded zero nonzero `NtReadFile` APC routines, so this run provides no evidence that Steel Battalion uses the APC path fixed by PR #226 and no upstream change was copied. The guest continued reading `snk_005.sgt` successfully through offset 8,716,288. Static disassembly maps the watchdog stack to `sub_000DFE75` calling `sub_0002DB50`; that guest helper polls shared producer/consumer counters at `0x00355730` and `0x00351F5C` until one item is available. Thus the sampled wait is a game-side ring-counter loop, not a deferred file APC. The trace did not capture those counter values, so it does not establish why the loop persisted or why presentation slowed. The temporary trace was removed and the regular Release executable rebuilt. Logs are under ignored `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-io-apc-trace-windowed-20261020/`. Next compare both counters and requested batch size at this helper in a title-reaching and a stalled run. Progress bars remain unchanged; title input acceptance, audible output, and gameplay remain unverified.

### 2026-10-20 follow-up: narrowed the startup stall

The temporary guest trace confirms the first wait in `sub_00216010` exits after one iteration when USER DMA GET reaches `0x4000`. It then records USER DMA PUT/GET advancing to `0x495C`. This corrects the earlier same-day note that treated the initial GET wait as still active: that wait is past; it is not the current blocker.

The subsequent queue-space wait is a separate path. In a bounded diagnostic trace the guest submits queue cursor 11 and reads completion value 3 from `0x80003000`; the 8-entry difference keeps the guest in `sub_00211E10` / `0x00211F10`. Prior executor-enabled captures show semaphore releases can advance that completion word, so a missed write to `0x80003000` is not established as the cause. Static review distinguishes the queue event `0x0021DCF4` from the nearby notification event `0x0021DCE4`; the generated vector-3 ISR/DPC and method callback can reach both through separate paths. No event was synthesized and no GPU interrupt behavior was changed.

Cross-checked the interrupt path against public Xbox sources. nxdk's pbkit installs a GPU ISR and deferred callback, then checks and acknowledges pending device sources; xemu models PGRAPH interrupt status as write-one-to-clear. This is useful behavioral reference, not proof of the exact Steel Battalion interrupt condition. The local Ghidra XBE analysis and xboxrecomp-generated guest code already provide the needed function/xref views, and Visual Studio 2022 Build Tools are installed; no additional decompiler or system dependency was installed. No upstream implementation code was copied. References: [nxdk pbkit ISR/DPC](https://github.com/XboxDev/nxdk/blob/master/lib/pbkit/pbkit.c), [xemu PGRAPH interrupt register behavior](https://github.com/xqemu/xqemu/blob/master/hw/xbox/nv2a/nv2a_pgraph.c), [Xbox XBE Ghidra loader](https://github.com/XboxDev/ghidra-xbe).

Removed the temporary probe from ignored generated source and rebuilt the Release x64 executable successfully with MSBuild. The follow-up diagnostic launch did not produce a separate watchdog artifact, so it adds no new timing result. No title screen, audio, input response, or gameplay was verified by this follow-up. Milestone 3 and all progress bars remain unchanged. Next: use the existing static cross-references and targeted runtime traces to determine which completed queue operation should signal `0x0021DCF4`, then test the smallest hardware-equivalent wakeup without signaling a game-specific event by address.

### 2026-10-20 renderer throughput profile and community research

Reviewed the current [Xemu-SB-VK Steel Battalion fork](https://github.com/quizerno/Xemu-SB-VK), its documented input/rebinding status, the community [Steel Battalion controller input guide](https://github.com/quizerno/Steel-Battalion-Entry-Guide/blob/main/3-Controller%20Inputs.md), and a public [XID report example](https://gist.github.com/Ryzee119/5b74e94eb765bcc54e68afde9ddc7623). The guide corroborates the 26-byte Steel Battalion input report layout. The XID example identifies the first report field as three 16-bit button words; XboxDevWiki search results independently list `RightJoyMainWeapon` at button word 2, mask `0x01`, matching the current profile’s first button bit. That supports the tested A-to-Main-Weapon report mapping; it does not prove the title's prompt handler consumes the report.

Ran a 120-second bounded profile with pushbuffer execution, async I/O, title-event bridging, synthetic AC'97/DSP acknowledgements, framebuffer capture, and `RECOMP_PB_TIMING`. The run reached the Nude Maker logo but not the Steel Battalion title. It presented about 0.65–0.70 frames per second. Repeated 512×512 DXT-textured CPU-raster draws took approximately 1.15–1.27 seconds each, while sampled NV2A `PUT` and `GET` were equal. This run therefore identifies CPU software rasterization as the measured startup throughput bottleneck; it does not prove why earlier runs reached the title or establish a hardware-renderer fix. The displayed frame is visibly dim, and no real audio or gameplay was verified.

Compared the default raster worker count (12 total on the 8-core/16-thread Ryzen 7 5700X3D) with an explicit 8-worker run under the same startup settings. The explicit run remained at the Nude Maker logo and about 0.66 frames per second; measured textured draws were roughly 1.07–1.16 seconds. This modest change does not resolve startup throughput and is not being made a default. That diagnostic was stopped after capturing enough samples. No runtime source change was made, and no game process remains.

Upstream XboxRecomp documentation recommends iterative function-level analysis and runtime profiling; the local checkout already has the maintained v0.13.1 tooling, Ghidra analysis, and Visual Studio 2022 build tools. No additional tool install or wholesale toolkit replacement was needed. The Xemu fork's own README describes inconsistent Steel Battalion campaign performance and unfinished direct joystick rebinding; it is useful community evidence and not a static-recompilation implementation to substitute here.

Milestone 3 and all progress bars remain unchanged. ### 2026-10-20 guest DMA wait and pushbuffer evidence

A 20-second watchdog run captured the guest at the same wait in `sub_00216010`: it compares the lower 28 bits of the USER DMA GET value at `0xFD800044` against `0x80004000` (the buffer base), while the runtime reports USER PUT=GET=`0x495C`. A local dump around the submitted segment tail (`0x8000491C`–`0x80004978`) contains no jump marker in those words. This narrows the problem to the difference between the title's expected return to the buffer base and the runtime's current “GET equals submitted PUT” acknowledgement; it does not yet establish the correct transition or prove this is the only boot blocker.

Checked upstream references before changing behavior: xemu's NV2A USER reads expose the PFIFO DMA_GET register, and its DMA pusher advances GET as it consumes command words and handles jump/call/return control flow ([xemu `user.c`](https://github.com/xemu-project/xemu/blob/master/hw/xbox/nv2a/user.c), [xemu `pfifo.c`](https://github.com/xemu-project/xemu/blob/master/hw/xbox/nv2a/pfifo.c)). nxdk's pushbuffer diagnostics also read the hardware DMA_GET register directly ([nxdk `pbkit.c`](https://github.com/XboxDev/nxdk/blob/master/lib/pbkit/pbkit.c)). No upstream code was copied. The current local worker still mirrors PUT to GET after its separate command scan, so faithful GET progression/control flow remains unimplemented. Temporary word-dump instrumentation was removed and the Release target rebuilt; no runtime source change remains. Milestone 3 and progress bars remain unchanged. Next model or trace the title's pushbuffer control flow and validate the wait transition, then rerun boot.

### Independent XBE analysis and upstream research (2026-10-19)

Reviewed the maintained [xboxrecomp toolkit](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp), its [v0.13.1 release](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/releases/tag/v0.13.1), the [Ghidra XBE loader](https://github.com/XboxDev/ghidra-xbe), and [xemu's Steel Battalion compatibility record](https://xemu.app/titles/43430002/). The local toolkit is already at v0.13.1 and Visual Studio is installed, so neither a toolkit upgrade nor a new system-wide compiler was needed. The upstream XBE-to-C pipeline includes Ghidra headless scripts; a local Ghidra 12.1.4 + JDK 21 install was used for an independent pass. All tool files and game-derived analysis remain local/ignored.

Imported a flat raw image of the user's XBE at image base `0x00010000`. Ghidra's focused auto-analysis completed successfully in 97 seconds. It independently identified instruction/function starts at `0x0002DB50`, `0x000DFF40`, `0x00211E10`, `0x0020DBC0`, `0x00216BE0`, and `0x00216EA0`, corroborating the existing function boundaries. Decompiled `0x000DFF40` calls `0x0002DB50(0x19)` (25 entries). `0x0002DB50` calls `0x0020EC60`, obtains the limit through `0x0020F2C0`, and waits in a predicate loop until `consumer + requested_count <= limit`. `0x00216BE0` has six call references and calls the XBE thunk at `0x0025D1E8`. This independently confirms the caller/request-size and callback path already under investigation; it does not explain why the runtime sometimes stops before the queue becomes available, nor justify a kernel/event change.

Keep this separate from the DCF4 queue-space wait: `0x00211E10` clears and waits on DCF4 when NV2A queue space is insufficient. The earlier title-reaching 120-second run's watchdog was waiting on DCF4 (`ESI=0x0021DCF4`, `ECX=0x0021B790`, USER `PUT=0x80F6C`, `GET=0x7C52C`). Correction: the `6` visible on the guest stack is the `WaitReason` argument to `KeWaitForSingleObject(event, 6, 1, 0, 0)`, not a six-entry request. Ghidra shows the actual queue-space helper compares the current pointer at `base+0x30`, the completion pointer at `base+0x34`, and the caller's target pointer; it also calls `0x00211AB0` to compute available space. The capture is not evidence that the DCE4 25-entry path stalled. The two helpers have different predicates, event words, and consumers; sample each against its own state.

The broad Ghidra pass with decompiler-wide parameter analysis was stopped after repeated expensive speculative-code warnings; no exports were lost because the targeted pass completed independently. Ghidra's optional Jython export script was unavailable, so the successful targeted report was generated with a local Java script. No decompiled game code, XBE, or asset was added to Git. Progress bars and Milestone 3 are unchanged.

The xemu compatibility report still rates Steel Battalion “Intro” based on a January 2022 report; linked graphics assertions concern emulator rendering and do not match the native runtime's observed queue wait. Stock xemu also documents no Steel Battalion controller mapping. This is contextual comparison only, not a diagnosis of this project's stall.

If it is `0x0002DB50`, trace DCE4 and its caller-specific consumer limit (the `0x000DFF40` path requests 25 entries). Keep instrumentation diagnostic-only until a trace identifies a missing publication or signal. Title input, audio output, gameplay, saving, and campaign progression remain unverified.

### DCE4 event and shared-ring state sample (2026-10-19)

A 120-second low-overhead run again remained on a black framebuffer and ended at the watchdog with the DCE4 wait frame (`0x0021DCE4`, return site `0x0020EC83` / `0x0002DB6A`); USER `PUT=GET=0x5EBD8`. External sampling showed the guest XInput packet counter stop at 1,593 and the USB report remain neutral. Across 26 corrected two-second samples from about 6 to 50 seconds, DCE4's guest signal-state word was `1`, ring field `0x351F58` was `2`, and `0x355730` and `0x351F5C` both held `1,254`. The ring count did not advance in that interval while the event remained signaled. The initial sampler used incorrect host addresses for the latter two fields; the corrected addresses add the runtime's `0x10000` guest-memory offset, and a direct process-memory read confirmed the same values.

This refines the diagnosis: a simple unsignaled DCE4 event is not supported by these samples. The ring appears at its observed limit while the event is signaled, but the current trace does not identify the field meanings beyond the generated code's accesses or the guest producer that should advance them. The watchdog stack can retain a wait frame and is not by itself proof that the guest was blocked inside the host wait at that exact instant. No title, title input, audio, or gameplay was verified. No source changes or instrumentation; no process remains. Logs/captures are local/ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-dce4-ring-state-120s-20261019/`; progress bars and M3 remain unchanged; user XID patch untouched.

### Startup queue and event-wait comparison (2026-10-19)

A 45-second run without pushbuffer execution repeated the known queue-space wait at `sub_00211E10` / `0x00211F10`; the native PC and stack identified that as the intentionally executor-disabled ring-buffer fence. A follow-up 90-second run enabled the existing pushbuffer executor, asynchronous file path, modeled vblank, title-event bridge, and synthetic AC'97 readiness. At timeout, the native stack ended in Windows `NtWaitForSingleObject`; guest registers show the wait on event `0x0021DCF4`, and NV2A `PUT=GET=0x73B24`. The event bridge logged creation of a host notification event. This captures a later wait after queue progress, but does not identify its missing/late producer.

The window was not captured for visual inspection in this run. The title screen, audible output, input acceptance, and gameplay remain unverified; APU interrupt logs do not prove audio output. The test used opt-in diagnostic behavior and does not validate those modes as defaults. No progress bars advance; Milestone 3 remains active.

### Event-state watch during later startup (2026-10-19)

A clean 60-second repetition enabled a change watch on the event's signal-state dword and wrote watchdog output to a separate file. The watch first observed state `1`, then state `0` before the guest's `KeWaitForSingleObject` call (ordinal 159). The watchdog's native stack confirms the guest was inside `bridge_KeWaitForSingleObject` → `xbox_KeWaitForSingleObject` → `WaitForSingleObjectEx`; the watched value was zero at wait entry and NV2A `PUT=GET=0x72680`. Other concurrent samples observed `1→0` across bridge calls, but do not identify which guest thread wrote the value. This verifies an event reset immediately before a host wait, not the event producer or why the wait is not released. The run logged 318 flips and 321 clears; no screenshot was collected for visual inspection, and title presentation remains unverified for this particular run.

A separate repeat without a dedicated watchdog log stopped updating its log while the process remained responsive at 0 FPS/draws 2. Three external memory samples read the event state as `1`; the process was stopped manually. Treat it as an unsuccessful capture attempt, superseded by the clean watchdog run above.

Next: trace which guest thread sets and resets `0x0021DCF8` around ordinal 159, and correlate that with the host event handle's signaled state before changing synchronization.

### Queue-event mapping and bridge trace (2026-10-19)

Static inspection separates two adjacent guest events. The queue-space helper `sub_00211E10` waits on object `0x0021DCF4` (global object `0x0021B790` + `0x2564`), whose signal-state dword is `0x0021DCF8`. Its blocking call is at guest return site `0x00211F3B`; the helper first checks the NV2A ring-buffer producer/completion counters and waits when space is insufficient. The nearby event `0x0021DCE4` (`+0x2554`, signal state `0x0021DCE8`) is a separate notification event used by `sub_0020EC60`. Earlier traces that grouped them as one event were ambiguous and should be read with this distinction.

A temporary, opt-in bridge trace captured the queue wait at call `156010`: caller `0x00211F3B`, guest signal state `0`, host event result `WAIT_TIMEOUT` (258), notification type, then `WaitForSingleObjectEx`. No `KeSetEvent` for `0x0021DCF4` appeared before that wait. An all-event trace changed startup timing and logged repeated sets for the separate `0x0021DCE4` event from caller `0x00216CAE`; it did not establish the queue event's producer.

After restoring the bridge source byte-for-byte and forcing a normal rebuild, an uninstrumented 75-second run stopped at the same queue-event wait. External reads showed producer counter `0x529`, completion pointer `0x80003000`, queue-event state `0`, and separate title-event state `1`. The process memory reader could not read the completion word through the high alias, so the producer/completion gap remained unmeasured in that run. The watchdog saw NV2A `PUT=GET=0x7274C`; renderer logs counted 322 flips, 692 draws and 1,384 triangles, but no screenshot was collected for visual inspection. No title-screen, audio, input, or gameplay result is verified.

A follow-up used the runtime's own guest-memory sampler with the pushbuffer executor, packet scan, async I/O, modeled vblank, title event bridge, and synthetic AC'97 readiness enabled. The sampler read guest `0x80003000` as `0xAB`, while the producer at `0x0021B7C0` was `0xB7`; the gap was 12 entries against the helper's 8-entry threshold. The guest was still in `sub_00211E10` (`EAX=8`, `ECX=0xAB`, `EDX=0x80003000`, `ESI=0xB7`, `EDI=0x0C`), with NV2A `PUT=0x128A0`, `GET=0x11E08`. Semaphore logs independently show releases being written at host `0x80013000`, the mapped address for guest `0x80003000` with the run's `0x10000` memory offset. This establishes that the completion word is readable through the runtime mapping and that the queue was behind by 12; it does not explain why DMA stopped advancing or verify a visible screen. The event state was 1 at this watchdog sample, but the active wait was the queue fence, not a host event wait.

The normal Release executable was rebuilt with the original bridge SHA-256 `2190BBD3E20DA13238802F655F198ECEE5BCC8A123CF6A774142C298F47CF232`; the temporary `EVENTTRACE` marker is absent. Both follow-up diagnostic runs are complete, and no game process remains. Keep progress bars unchanged; Milestone 3 stays active.

Next: inspect the current GET packet window and measure the executor's per-method cost; the GET value is only updated after the submitted segment finishes.

### Pushbuffer GET-window inspection (2026-10-19)

A temporary watchdog-only dump captured the current GET window in a matched 90-second executor run. At the watchdog, USER `GET=0x12DEC`, `PUT=0x13DE8`; the first dword at GET was `0x00081D98`, an increasing packet for `SET_CLEAR_RECT_HORIZONTAL` and `SET_CLEAR_RECT_VERTICAL`, with full-screen bounds `0x027F0000` and `0x01DF0000`. The following words form ordinary NV097 clear/depth and state packets. An earlier sample at `GET=0x11E08` contained the same valid packet sequence, and the later sample had advanced to `0x12DEC`; this rules out a fixed malformed header at `0x11E08`.

The watchdog can observe a pending submission before the executor finishes it: the runtime updates USER GET to PUT only after `nv2a_pb_scan` executes the submitted segment. Thus a GET-window dump identifies the queue's last reported completion point, not necessarily the exact command executing on the worker thread. The same run reported 22 segments and 14,210 scanned words with no unrecognised words, 41 flips at 0.66 reported FPS, 80 vertex-program batches and about 24.6 million pixel writes. It had no textured batches in the measured summary and no visual capture; the low rate is a bring-up performance measurement, not an accurate gameplay benchmark.

The dump hook was removed immediately after collection, the normal Release target rebuilt successfully, and the test process ended at its watchdog. Logs and the GET-window sample remain local/ignored. Progress bars remain unchanged; Milestone 3 stays active.

A separate 60-second `RECOMP_PB_TIMING` run measured the latest submitted segment (`0x80005F40`–`0x800069D8`, 678 words). Method `0x17FC` (`SET_BEGIN_END`) accounted for 2,678.572 ms across eight packets; the next reported method total was below 1 ms. In the runtime, closing `SET_BEGIN_END` calls `draw_primitive` for these buffered batches, and the same report counted 12 vertex-program batches and 48 vertices. This localizes the largest measured cost to the CPU draw path, rather than to the clear-rectangle packet seen at GET. The 60-second run reached 19 flips at 0.53 reported FPS and ended at the watchdog; it did not verify the title screen, audio, input, or gameplay. These are bring-up measurements, not gameplay benchmarks.

Next: trace the `draw_primitive` program path and compare its CPU raster workload against an exact small reference scene before optimizing it; preserve framebuffer output and command ordering.

### Latest bounded title and Steel Battalion USB input probe (2026-10-18)

A follow-up 180-second run kept the `RECOMP_PAD_LIVE` input file empty so no button was sent before the title prompt appeared. It stopped at the Dolby startup image and produced no new framebuffer captures after the first 44 seconds. The watchdog sampled guest stack pointer `0x00F7FE18`, registers `EAX=0x00F7FE60`, `ESI=0x80100080`, and NV2A USER `PUT=GET=0x645FC`; the process exited at its configured bound. This run neither reached the title nor tested game-side input. The stalled path is not yet explained.

A matched 120-second control with the same USB and graphics settings but no live-input watcher stopped at the same Dolby image. At timeout the guest snapshot again showed ESP `0x00F7FE18`; the queue had advanced to `PUT=0x38F88`, `GET=0x384F0`. This shows the watcher is not required for the startup stall. A second matched run with asynchronous-I/O mode disabled stopped on a black frame with `PUT=GET=0x495C`, so that toggle also did not recover startup. A temporary trace of the guest's repeated wait at return address `0x00191817` recorded a relative delay of `-660000` 100-nanosecond units (66 ms), confirming a normal polling sleep rather than an infinite wait. The temporary trace was removed and the ordinary Release executable rebuilt; no runtime is left running. These runs do not reach the title or verify audio/input.

An opt-in file trace confirmed that the guest opens handles with `CreateOptions=0x48` (asynchronous under the current runtime's classification) and issues `NtReadFile` requests of 133,120 bytes with no event or APC. The bridge completes the data read synchronously, writes the I/O status block, and returns `STATUS_PENDING` for those handles. Static XBE analysis shows the caller branches on `STATUS_PENDING` and calls `NtWaitForSingleObject` on the file handle. However, a targeted runtime trace did not capture that call at the expected guest call sites, so file-object wait behavior remains unverified and is not yet a confirmed cause of the Dolby stall. A later 120-second run still stopped at Dolby after reading `STRM00.WAV` through offset 262,144; its watchdog snapshot and framebuffer did not establish a deadlock. Temporary instrumentation was removed and the normal Release target rebuilt with no trace markers.

A combined 60-second run enabled both read and native-wait tracing. It observed asynchronous file opens, but neither the pending-read trace nor the low-level `NtWaitForSingleObject` trace fired. The guest continued sequential `READ` calls from `0x0018E95A` through `STRM00.WAV` and stopped at the same Dolby frame. This means the async-handle hypothesis from the separate trace was not reproduced in the actual stalled stream path; do not change file completion behavior based on it. All temporary hooks were removed and the ordinary Release executable rebuilt successfully.

Static disassembly confirms `0x0018E95A` is immediately after `NtReadFile` in a read/write stream-copy loop, distinct from the `0x0018E0AF` helper that branches on `STATUS_PENDING`. In the frozen-run log, the reader requested 65,536-byte chunks with no event/APC and each recorded call returned `STATUS_SUCCESS` with all 65,536 bytes. Successful-title and frozen-run logs both show reads at the initial offsets, but later bytes at matching offsets differ and the existing trace does not identify the handle/path or guest thread for each read. The title-reaching run enters heap cleanup after about 589,824 bytes, while the frozen run continues farther. The pending-file-wait theory therefore does not explain the observed successful reads. The next trace should include thread, handle/path, offset, and read/write status alongside frame/queue progress to establish where the runs diverge; no cause is confirmed.

A temporary thread/handle/path trace did not reach the filtered `0x0018E95A`/`0x0018E982` read/write calls. It logged the audio worker opening sequential `D:\Media\title\snk_*.sgt` and `.wav` assets, then the game crashed with `STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION` at guest VA `0xFFFFFFF4` in `sub_0021ED29`, called from `sub_0021FE7C` while walking an audio-object list under guest critical section `0x0023FE18`. A matched run after removing the trace hooks reproduced the same fault address and guest function, so the crash is not attributable to trace output.

A follow-up trace confirmed the values: startup caller `0x001108AF` entered wrapper `sub_00108CF0` with object `0x006C1E80`, whose first field was zero; the wrapper passed that zero as the object argument to `sub_00221B19`. That reached `sub_0021FE7C` with object and list head both zero, then attempted node zero minus `0x14`, producing `0xFFFFFFEC`; the callee's read at `[arg+8]` faulted at `0xFFFFFFF4`. XBE disassembly shows constructor `sub_00108420` initializes the first field of the object at `0x006C1E80` to zero. The bad list walk is confirmed, but the intended meaning of that field and why the startup path calls through it while null remain unverified. No behavior was patched. All temporary stream and generated-code trace hooks were removed, generated sources restored byte-for-byte from local backups, and Release x64 rebuilt successfully. Next: trace the guest code that should populate the `0x006C1E80` field and identify the expected audio object/list initialization sequence before changing behavior.

A 30-second bounded write-watch run reproduced the same access violation at `0xFFFFFFF4` in `sub_0021ED29`. The configured watch on the page containing `0x006C1E80` yielded no recorded value-changing write before the crash; because the run log does not preserve an arm marker or distinguish writes of zero, this is inconclusive about all writes to the field. It does not establish that initialization failed in this run: the captured log has no `DirectSoundCreate`/constructor HRESULT. Earlier traces did record `DSERR_NODRIVER` when the AC'97 ready status was absent, while other startup captures recorded successful constructor results, so initialization must be correlated with the crashing run before assigning the current crash to that failure mode. No runtime behavior was changed.

A paired bounded run with `RECOMP_AC97_READY=1` showed why that option cannot be assessed alone: without `RECOMP_PB_EXEC`, the guest stayed in the early GPU queue-space poll at `sub_00211E10`, with DMA PUT=GET=`0x495C`, and the semaphore trace saw method packets but no executor-side release. With the pushbuffer executor enabled, it executed three semaphore releases to guest VA `0x80003000` with values `5`, `7`, and `9`, and initialized the local APU model. The guest bootstrapped 2,048 program words each for GP and EP DSPs, then opened `dsstdfx.bin`, `snk_000.sgt/.wav` through `snk_009.sgt/.wav`, and `capcom.xpr`. Windowed captures at 85 and 140 seconds remained black with one framebuffer flip and zero guest draw batches; the watchdog sampled the known 66 ms delay path (`sub_00191864`, called from `sub_000C83C0` → `sub_000DFE90` → `sub_000467A0`). A 165-second matched run also enabled the virtual Steel Battalion USB profile: OHCI processed more than 25,000 periodic transfers, but the guest still had one flip, zero draw batches, and the same wait path. By comparison, the saved 180-second title-reaching run used the APU and USB paths and had 261 flips and 574 draws before its title capture. A 180-second Vulkan launch with the virtual Steel Battalion USB profile reached the dark title screen and displayed “PRESS MAIN WEAPON BUTTON”. After that prompt appeared, the live input file sent `a:500`. The OHCI report trace captured a 26-byte XID report with byte 2 bit 0 set (`00 1A 01 ...`) and then cleared (`00 1A 00 ...`); OHCI continued periodic transfers, reaching 12,795 before the watchdog. This verifies that the virtual USB device emitted the intended press and release packets. It does **not** verify that the guest recognized the control: title captures before, during, and after the pulse were byte-identical (SHA-256 `604A1D53DDD97989B4688868E7ECFB5E0F2F1ED88DE1CAF6FC7A6C72F1F83F4E`).

The watchdog ended the earlier process at 180 seconds with the guest waiting at event `0x0021DCF4`, 955,910 indirect calls, and NV2A USER `PUT=GET=0x6D11C`. This is an idle/wait snapshot, not proof of a deadlock. Temporary XID-report and guest file-helper trace hooks were removed, then the normal Release target rebuilt successfully. The XInput A-to-`RightJoyMainWeapon` mapping remains unverified at the game level and is still an uncommitted local patch. The ISO, generated source, logs, and screenshots remain local/ignored. No progress bars advance; Milestone 3 remains active.

`nv2a_pb_exec` sends one tightly checked textured splash-logo draw to a project-owned GPU pipeline; six nonblack fade frames match CPU-reference captures byte-for-byte. Other pushbuffer draws still use the CPU reference rasterizer. Multiple bounded runs displayed very dark Steel Battalion title art and a faint “PRESS MAIN WEAPON BUTTON” prompt. A USB report trace verified the virtual device emitted a press and release packet, but identical before/during/after captures did not show a guest response. Other runs with live or scripted input enabled stopped advancing earlier at the Nude Maker or Dolby screens. The cause of those stalls is not established, so do not attribute them to USB or the A-button mapping. General graphics, audio, and gameplay remain unverified. Milestone 3 remains active.

The latest bounded diagnostic launch rebuilt successfully with the Vulkan path and isolated watchdog logging. It exited at 45 seconds with code 3 after 171 flips; sampled NV2A `PUT` and `GET` were equal at `0x6AAB8`. Startup read `STRM00.WAV` and `STRM01.WAV`; the guest stack contained the return address from a `KeWaitForSingleObject` wrapper. This does not establish why the guest was waiting, verify audible output, or reach the Steel Battalion title screen. The watchdog's separate log path is a local diagnostic change and has not been added to the dependency patch stack.

A follow-up 60-second run added timer-thread stage markers. They continued through `before/after-vblank`, `before/after-irq`, and `before/after-dpc` at loop 3000; the isolated watchdog ended the process at 60 seconds. This rules out a timer-thread stall in those three stages for that run. The guest still had not reached the title screen, and the run does not establish why game-thread progress stopped. Diagnostics remain local and ignored.

A further 60-second bounded launch traced the guest-built event at `0x0021DCE4`. All 128 logged `KeWaitForSingleObject` calls returned `STATUS_SUCCESS`; the title repeatedly reset the notification event and another runtime thread signaled it. The NV2A queue continued to drain and reached `PUT=GET=0x4E81C`; the run logged 400 draw batches and 200 clears before the watchdog. The watchdog stack happened to sample the event wrapper, which does not prove a deadlock. No title-screen, audio-output, or gameplay verification was made, and no progress bars advance. Tracing, logs, and the rebuilt binary are local under the ignored `iso/local-analysis/` and `local-tools/` paths.

A separate 45-second capture run enabled per-draw timing and saved the first 16 vertex-program draw results locally. The 16th capture visibly shows the dim CAPCOM logo and is byte-identical to three earlier Vulkan captures; it is not an exact match to a CPU-reference capture from a different fade timing. This does not change the prior six-state CPU/Vulkan parity result. The diagnostic timing recorded 63 Vulkan logo submissions averaging 16.31 ms and 16 black-depth operations averaging 1.35 ms; these are instrumented timings, not performance benchmarks. The run reached 51 flips and stopped at the guest queue fence with 12 commands outstanding. It produced no generic `cpu-raster` batches, so the sample did not reach the unsupported draw state targeted for profiling. No title-screen or gameplay progress is established. Captures and logs remain local under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-vulkan-program-draw-capture-45s-20261018/`.

See [the Vulkan migration map](VULKAN_MIGRATION_MAP.md) for source call paths, verified runtime boundaries, and the remaining implementation steps.

The XBE entry point and section map are documented; that is static analysis, not verified runtime behavior. The title-asset bar records a diagnostic read of the three title XPR files only, not complete filesystem compatibility. Pushbuffer handling records command processing and draw calls, not a correct rendered scene. A fresh local build completed and the XBE reached its entry point. An opt-in NV2A trace then observed display-mode setup and GPU semaphore releases before the process exited on an access violation in the XBE's audio cleanup path. It again recorded `DSERR_NODRIVER`, a null audio vtable, and invalid audio virtual calls. This corroborates the already traced failed-DirectSound cleanup path; it does not establish that the GPU caused the crash or that untraced startup now has deterministic behavior. With diagnostic AC'97 readiness and a fake DSP doorbell acknowledgement, the guest read additional resources and flipped three frames, but the captured output was black and the renderer found no usable textured batches. The fake acknowledgement clears commands without DSP computation, so this run does not verify DSP or audio behavior. The host DirectSound COM wrapper is a no-output stub and the generated game code uses its XBE-resident DirectSound implementation; neither is a verified audio path. The APU has a Windows XAudio2 output backend, but its GP/EP DSP is stubbed. The optional DSP56300 FFI bridge and its synthetic memory-map/lifecycle harness now compile in an isolated worktree, and the harness passes. The patch does not route GP/EP MMIO or execute DSP firmware, and it does not change the audio status bars. AC'97 readiness, DSP processing, safe audio cleanup, audible output, and a verified title screen remain unverified. These diagnostic hooks remain opt-in. The campaign bars remain at zero because no mission has been verified from loading through completion and progression.

#### Latest bounded rebuild and pixel trace (2026-10-17)

The isolated `RelWithDebInfo` game target rebuilt successfully with the installed Visual Studio CMake. A 15-second watchdog-bounded launch using the existing diagnostic AC'97-ready and fake DSP-ack options reached guest entry `0x00192C47`, read game resources, and produced a framebuffer capture. The capture was not a recognizable title screen; the trace still logged unusable texture batches and a mostly black output. Pixel tracing sampled the full-screen textured quad: texture samples and RGB output were nonzero, while diffuse alpha and final combiner alpha were zero. A vertex-program dump showed that the XBE's inline data supplied diffuse alpha `0` and its vertex program directly copied that attribute to the color output. With source-alpha blending enabled (`0x302/0x303`), the transparent quad correctly leaves the black destination unchanged. This rules out the current vertex interpreter inventing alpha zero for this draw, but it does not establish whether the game should advance to a later screen or change the submitted fade alpha. No renderer fix was applied. Diagnostic audio shortcuts remain in effect, so DSP execution and audible output are still unverified. No progress bars advance, and no title-screen or campaign compatibility is claimed.

#### Latest bounded DSP effects-image trace (2026-10-17)

The Windows target built successfully with a temporary local-only trace inside the translated DSOUND-section function. A watchdog-bounded launch read all 24,936 bytes of `Media\SndEff\DSSTDFX.BIN`; the function received the buffer and returned `S_OK`. Static tracing shows that the XBE-resident DSOUND path builds 32-byte mapping records, relocates GP-memory references, copies mapped image data, then writes and polls a command word. This identifies the guest-side transaction shape, but not the meaning of every record or whether the DSP accepted or processed it. A corrected 8-second launch observed GP scratch registers `GPSADDR=0x003C4000`/`GPSMAXSGE=206`, EP `EPSADDR=0x003DC000`/`EPSMAXSGE=19`, and nonzero frame-table registers. The actual GP/EP SGE tables and first mapped payload are in the `0x80000000` contiguous alias: a write watch caught the first GP entry and the watchdog sample showed a physical page address there. Earlier low-alias-only zero reads and addresses `0xFE832040`/`0xFE8320D4`, `0xFE852048`/`0xFE8520DC` were invalid. Synthetic AC'97 readiness and fake DSP doorbell acknowledgement were enabled, so `S_OK` does not verify DSP execution, effects, audible output, or the title screen. xemu's GP/EP reference uses the descriptor registers for scatter/gather DMA and bootstraps P memory on reset release. The project APU's physical resolver already selects the contiguous alias, but the DSP scratch/bootstrap callback is missing. xemu's 512-byte MCPX boot ROM is mapped in the CPU BIOS region and not passed to its APU initializer, so it is not established as DSP firmware. Next implement and synthesize-test the callback and reset bootstrap. Audio and all playable-feature bars remain unchanged.
